DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
June 19, 1940
In reference to Tokyo's telegram 456,
June 18, noon, FE understands that you have
undertaken tentative conversations with
Amerioan broadcasting interests, the outcome
of which you will wish to incorporate in
a telegram to Tokyo in reply to its 456.
With regard to that part of telegram 456
which has to do with the proposal that Am-
bassador Grew participate in the broadcast,
FE suggests that the following be incorporated
in the Department's reply:
The Department approves your
accepting the invitation to participate
in the proposed broadcast in tribute to
the late Prince Tokugawa along the lines
which you suggest in your 456. With
regard to the advance transmission to
the Department of a draft of your
remarks, such procedure would permit
us to give the American press accurate
information and to offer any suggestions
which the Department might have.
MR.

FILE
I respectfully call the Department's attention
to the enclosed copy of the bulletin No. 48 for June
1940 of the Broadcasting Corporution of Japan which
should be interesting to the Department for the fol-
lowing reasons:
1. On page 2 of the bulletin is an interestine
LIAISSN OFICE
ief history of the development of the Japanese short
broadcasting to foreign countries.

3. On pages 2 and 1l there are indications of the
extent to which the Japanese Government through the
Broadcasting Corporation of Japan is using American

Japanese propaganda.
4. On page 1l, attention is called to additional
programs directed to Hawaii and the Near East.
As in other issues of the monthly bulletin, the great
emphasis placed upon propaganda thrcueh broadcasts of
news and "culture" is impressive.

as to whether the Consulate should continue to report upon
this subject.

5
日まで頼いて來ました。この町の界
からでした。さらいふ市民の心が今
線にある將士等の勞苦を思ひゃる心
想つて見て下さいそれらは皆、戦
あるわたしどもの抑制と忍耐とをも
た。きう申しては何ですが、銃後に
れのみを待つてみたやらなもので！
乾き切つた寒い冬籠りの間唯そ
年來の冬季の乾燥だと昔はれたばど
たしどもは四ケ月も前かいー六ト
てるた心持はありませんでした。わ
か來るかと思つてこの雨を待ち佗び
ばすとはこの雨のことでせら。来る
らになりました。古人のいふ蓬を伸
のやらな、暖かい雨がやつて来るや
最早、事礎以來三目の春の先隅
牡丹などもさうです。牡丹は支明の
は花真術許りでなくさくらさうや
ふことが出来るのでありますこれ
れた技備と鑑識を持つて居たかを窺
が寝かで、花を作り鑑賞する事に優
我々の祖先が如何に花に對する趣味
ずの間に、殿演ーた事を考へますと
山の花高蒲の品種が僅か一百年足ら
ます。
本の花書浦に優るものはないと信じ
の種類がありませうがおそらく日
られます。外図には澤山のアイリス
形や色形が極めて多様たものか見
ではん百種以上の品種が作くて
只種の野生の花為消から、貝今
頃から流行したものですが、最初は
ます。その花序浙は德川幕府の末明
本でなければ見られない特色であり
かに四方に乗れて喚くところは、
ぼい大気の中にあって花遊が伸び
花でありまして、あの入梅頃のれ
て居りますが、此に初夏を代たく
た花で、今は海外も澤山招介され
月などの季筋に向ひます。
も終りを告げ、これから花高蒲やれ
木である、櫻や山茶、牡丹、藤など
一かもこの五百種以上を敗へる澤
花高浦は純や日本のみ發率
只今故國日本では春の代表的な花
日本の初夏の花
母國の春
ものは殆んど行りますまい。
を持ち來したいと願ってみなかった
の春にふさはーい延びノとーた力
ふものどうかして紀元二千六百年
たる寒さの底から起ち上りたいと願
一精にやって来たのです。この襟然
無象を促卜曠世の展望とが、殆んと
ない非常時の別ーい寒さとたきい
みなかいたものはないやうでーた。
人でも、子供でも、この作を待い
もが町で行き遂公留守居の女でもだ
て見ることし出来まさんわたーと
微だけでも戦地方ぬるの
局崎滕村
種あまりの様々六僕壁りか見いれま
がありま頭に
一く喉く答椎にも百ぶ一種もの品種

=== Page 46 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to despatch
No. 4817 dated July 6, 1940,
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Broadcast to the United States from Station JoAk, Tokyo,
July 5, 1940. - A tribute to the late Prince Iyesato
Tokugawa.
By Joseph C. Grew
Just one month ago there passed from this distraught
world of ours an outstanding figure in the life of Japan,
a statesman, humanitarian and great gentleman, Prince
Iyesato Tokugawa, whose memory we honor today in this
brief broadcast from Tokyo to the United States. Indeed
our tribute is of direct interest to Americans for a good
many reasons but partioularly because during the later
years of his long and useful life the Prince devoted an
important part of his manifold interests and activities
toward developing the iendship between Japan and the
United States. Often during the past eight years I have
had the privilege of sitting with him at lunchoons and din-
ners of the America-Japan Society whose presidency he
filled with greet charm and great distinction and have
felt with pleasure the genuine warmth of his greeting to
our fellow countrymen and women who were visiting Japan,
some of them for the first time. Many Americans, I am
sure, will recall with gratification the cordial spirit of
welcome which characterized those meetings.
You will be particularly inerested in hearing in a
few moments from the next speaker the dramatin story of
Prince Tokugawa's youth. He was, at least in name, the
last

=== Page 47 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
last of the Shoguns, those warrior chieftains who ruled
Japan for 250 years until the advent of the Meiji era in
1867, the last ruling Shogun having surrendered his ad-
ministration to the Emperor when Prince Iyesato (then
known as Kamenosuke) was but five years old, and as head
of the great house of Tokugawa this youth, with his bril-
liant ancestral background, found himself in maturer years
charged with important opportunities and responsibilities
for service to his nation. Few men could have fulfilled
that service with more patriotic spirit, determination
and effectiveness, and the demands for suoh service steadily
increased as his useful life progressed and his talents
developed.
Throughout his eminent career Prince Tokugawa's use-
fulness to his country was greatly enhanced through his
public school education in England where he attended Suy-
Cenham College, through his facile grasp of foreign languages,
especially English, and through his frequent visits to the
United States on important missions.
This background gave him an international outlook and
a deep understanding of foreign relations which proved to
be important assets in his presidency over a period of thirty
years of the Japanese House of Peers in the Imperial Diet.
But the phases of his public service of greatest interest
to Americans were first his presidency of the America-
Japan Society of Tokyo, second his presidency of the Japanese
Red Cross, and third his participation in the Washington
Conference of 192l where his knowledge of the English lan-
guage and his understanding of western temperament and ideals
proved

=== Page 48 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
proved to be of outstanding importance to the Japanese
delegation. It was, in the end, his conscientious spirit
of service that indirectly cost him his life because, in spite
of not too robust health, he insisted on traveling in l938
to the Red Cross Conference in London. On the way to London
he was taken ill in Canada and was reluctantly obliged to
return to Japan where the shock of his illness was too great
to overcome. His death ceme peacefully on June 5th.
Thus passed a great personality who has left an im-
perishable mark in the annals of his country. Although
sohooled in the ways and the thought of the West, his
ideas and views and interests were fundamentally Japanese.
He was a loyal Buddhist. He was a keen follower of Sumo
or Japanese wrestling which approrimates in public interest
our American interest in baseball and seldom was absent from
his seat at the big annual tournaments. He loved the Japa-
nese classic dence. In character he was reluctant to yield
in controversies but his sympathies were broad and they
found expression in many charitable activities. For his
contribution to American-Japanese relations I shall alvays
be deeply grateful. My admiration, respect and affection
for him were in the highest degree heart-felt. His passing
means a great and personal loss to all of us who knew him
well. The United States has lost a true friend.
It now gives me particular pleasure to introduce to
you my good friend Viscount Tadashro Inouye, a member of
the House of Peers and President of the Pan-Pacific Club of
Tokyo. Viscount Inouye will tell you something of Prince
Tokugawa's youth and life.

=== Page 49 === (confidence 0.99)
Enolosure No. 2 to despatoh
No. 4817 dated July 6, 1940,
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
A Tribute to the Late Prince Tokugawa
broadcast to the United States from
JOAK, July 5, 1940, by Viscount
Inouye.
Ladies and Gentlemen of the United States:
I have the honor of telling you something about the
illustrious personage who passed away just a month ago
today, and whose loss brought profound sorrow not only to
the entire Japanese nationa, but also to many prominent
persons elsewhere in the world.
Prince Tokugawa was not essentially a stateaman but
a personality of perhaps greater value and wider appeal
to the best portion of humanity. He was pre-eminently
a gentleman, and his noble character is appreciated
universally, regardless of race or nationality.
He came of the best blood of Japan and of a lineage
second to none among the country's illustrious families.
In both his lineage and his personal character he truly
represented a family of distinguished statesmen and bril-
liant leaders who have embellished the pages of our history.
In mourning the passing of Prince Tokugawa from our
midst, we are compelled to renew our sense of deep appre-
ciation for the great and noble services to our Empire
which were rendered by the illustrious Tokugawa Shoguns
during a period of two hundred and sirty years.
One thing for whioh Japan is particularly indebted
to the Tokugawa family is that, at the right moment in history,
that family readily and voluntarily restored to the Throne
the

=== Page 50 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
the administration of the entire country, thereby averting
dangerous and perhaps disastrous civil wars.
At the time of the Meiji Restoration of 1868, the
Prince was a boy of five years old. Fortunately for him-
self and his country he was surrounded from his infancy
by a group of able and loyal advisers and instructors who
were former followers of the Shogunate Government. He
received his early instruction in Oriental classios and
philosophy from those scholars who gave him a difficult
and thorough training in intellectual as well as moral
development.
Besides his Japanese eduoation, he received early in-
struction in English, first from an American citizen, Mr.
Warren Clark, and later a Canadian, Mr. Coohrane.
In l877 at the age of fourteen, Prince Tokugawa was
sent to England to study, and entered Sydenhem College
near London. He studied under the personal supervision of
the Prinoipal of the College, Mr. Taylor Jones, who used
to hold college debates at which many illustrious states-
men of the time, such as Mr. Gladstone and Lord Salisbury
were invited to inspire young students. This introduction
to the rules of parliamentary procedure gave the Prince his
early interest in the sphere of activity in which he later
distinguished himself as President of the House of Peers of
Japan.
After staying five years in England, Prince Tokugawa
returned to Japan, and in l896, he became the member of
the House of Peers. Sinoe then, for fifty years he was
active in the Upper House, of which the last thirty years
as

=== Page 51 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
as its President. There he won universal confidence and
respect for his ertraordinary ability and efficient manner
in handling parliamentary affairs.
In view of his unusual parliamentary ability and his
keen interest in national affairs, many attempts were made
to induce him to enter the arena of party politios. On
one occasion, the Emperor Taisho offered him the post of
Prime Minister, but Prince Tokugawa declined the honor.
The Prince received the Imperial command to attend the
historio Arms Conference at Washington in l92l. He valued
most highly the opportunity thus given him to make acquaint-
ance of many eminent statesmen of the world, and he had
high regard and admiration for Mr. Charles E. Hughes, now
Chief Justice of the United States.
The most outstanding characteristics of Prince Tokugawa
were patience and self-control, and his retentive memory
was most conspicuous. The strictness with which he regulated
his own life won the admiration of all who knew him, and
yet he was generous to others.
One of the many public organizations to which Prince
Tokugawa devoted his activities - and one in which he was
especially interested - was the Red Cross Society of Japan
of which he was President until his death. Because of his
personal efforts, cordial relations have always existed
between the Red Cross Society of America and of Japan. In
1930 on his way to the Conference of the International Red
Cross Societies at Brussels, Prince Tokugawa accidentally
met in a hotel in Paris the late Honorable John Burton Payne,
President of the Amerioan Red Cross Sooiety. The friendship
thus

=== Page 52 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
thus started between the distinguished American and Japanese
became so cordial that Judge Payne supported Prince Tokugawa
at the Brussels Conference and waived in favor of Japan
Amerioa's right as the scene of the next International Red
Cross Conference. Thus, in the fall of 1934 when the 15th
Conference of International Red Cross Societies was held
in Tokyo Judge Payne attended the Conference with a large
American delegation of sixty-six members. The sudden death
of Judge Payne after his return to the United States was
deeply lamented by Prince Tokugawa, and he held a memorial
service in Tokyo in honor of his American friend, which was
attended by a large assembly of many representative Japanese
oitizens.
Prince Tokugawa was interested in the cause of humanity
and international peace. In no undertaking was he more
keenly interested than in the promotion of international
friendship and mutual understanding between Japan and the
United States of America. For many years he was President
of the America-Japan Society. At all times, he was glad
to welcome visitors from across the Pacific. He was great
admirer of Abraham Lincoln, exponent of American ideals.
His dominant desire was to keep the permanent peaoe and
tranquility of the Pacific Ocean by the joint efforts of
Japan and the United States, and to that end he devoted his
unstinted services.
Alas! this great statesman of Japan has passed away.
Born to the illustrious house cf the Shoguns, Prinoe Tokugawa
worked whole-heartedly for the progress of his country, and
for international goodwill. He was a true patriot, e sincere
friend

=== Page 53 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
friend of America, and an ardent lover of humanity. He
was pre-eminently a great nobleman of international fame.
At any time we could ill afford to lose a man of such
superior character, and especially at this time of interna-
tional crisis, the loss of Prince Tokugawa is keenly felt,
and deeply lamented by our countrymen.
THE END.

Yokohama's despatch no. 666, June 29,
1940, "Compulsory Sealing of Radio Trans-
mitters and Receiving Sets on Board Ships
in Yokohama Harbor".
The Consul reports that he has been
informed by the Japanese official who seals
radio sets aboard ships in port that this
step 1s taken under authority of a general
regulation, but that sealing of sete is not
speoifically authorized. The official in
question is quoted as inquiring whether the
Únited States would take similar steps in
reprisal.
Comment: While it appears unlikely that
any strong objection to this practice could
be made by the Government under international
law, the Japanese practice under reference
might be borne in mind if en occasion arises
on which this Government decides to bring
pressure on the Japanese through retaliatory
measures.

I have the honor to report that the representative
in Yokohama of the Japanese Department of Coruunications
since the outbreak of the war in Europe last September
A-
has requircd the sealins of all radio transmitting
equipment aboard foreign merehant ships in the harbor.
This is accomplished by sealing the door to the ship's
radio room as soon as the ship enters the port and the
seals may not be broken before the ship departs.
In addition to souling the transmitting equipment
the local authorities are now scaling all receiving
equipment which officers, crew or pussengers may have
unless such equipment is exclusively lone wave. The
local authorities state that since on a single ship there
may be as many as 25 short wave receiving sets, rather
than seal individual sets the practice has been to carry
as many of the sets as possible into the radio room and
seal
F/N

=== Page 56 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
seal them all together. If a given set is too large
to carry conveniently it is sealed in place.
The matter was brought to the attention of the
Consulate recently by a ship captain who feared that the
authorities would remove his radio from his room. As his
receiving set was a permanent installation he was afraid
he would be put to considerable expense and trouble if
his set were to be removed from his room. The authorities
explained thut in such a cuse the sst would be sealed in
place.
The authorities went on to say that when a large ship
suc: as the S.S.PIESIDENT COOLIDGE is in port for a few
hours no attempt is mude to find and seal every receiving
set about the ship since such a course would take much
too long. The authorities cluim that they lhave no wish
to cause unnecussury disturbance but feel that such pre-
cautions are necessury due to the present uisturbed inter-
national situtt:on.
It is interesting to note thut the regulations
governing this matter do not proviue specificaily for
sealine reesivinr sets abo rd ships in port. The local
official was thy refore urable to uote anyth int but a
general regulation authorizing him to tuke necessary steps
under certain conditions.
The lccal official suid he r ulized that similur
measures were not followed in ull countries and wondered

reprisul.

A Tribute to the
Late Prince Iyesato Tokugawa
By His Excellency JOSEPH C. GREW.
Imbassador of the l'mited States to Japan.
Just one month ago there passed from this distraught
world of ours an outstanding figure in the life of Japan,
a statesman, humanitarian and great gentleman, Prince
Ivesato Tokugawa, whose memory we honor today in
this brief broadcast from Tokyo to the United States.
Indeed our tribute is of direct interest to Americans for
a good many reasons but particularly because during the
later years of his long and useful life the Prince devoted
an important part of his manifold interests and activities
toward developing the friendship between Japan and
the United States. Often during the past cight years 1
have had the privilege of sitting with him at luncheons
and dinners of the America-Japan Society whose presi-
dency he filled with great charm and great distinction
and have fel: with pleasure the genuine warmth of his
greeting to our fellow coun rymen and women who were
visiting Japan, some of them for the first time. Many
Americans, I am sure, will recall with gratification the
cordial spirit of welcome which characterized those
meetings.
You will be particularly interested in hearing in a
few moments from the next speaker the dramatic story
of Prince Tokugawa's youth. He was, at least in name,
the last of the Shoguns, those warrior chieftains who

determimation and etiectiveness, and the demands for

 keen follower of Sumo or Japanese wrestling which

中華民國國民政府宣傳部長
中華民國訪日答禮使節
林柏生先生的
演
講（五月二十四日）
黨的俘此、孫先生叫國家於革命破坏
蘇俄一回兒反共、一回兒甘心做共產
來覆去一回兒倚頼歌美一回兒投降
本合作、恢復亞細亞的地位、他却是
先生叫我們學日本的榜樣、並且與日
權位全不知道國家民族的利益。孫
了各位愛國的備胞、他祇知道個人的
背叛了中國々民寫因爲蔣介石背叛
爲蔣介石背叛了孫先生因爲蔣介石
木湖水裡頭的月影。爲什麼呢？因
烈期望的也都成了塊子裡頭的花
如今呢？載事拖了三年各位所熱
了、孫先生的主張一點沒會實現
住國家民族的自由可是北伐成功
爭終有和平解決之一日可以保得
人的欺負爲的是希望不得已之戰
做一個獨立自由的國民、不再受白種
內可以安居樂業各位在海外也可以
由的新中國、各位的兄弟姉妹、在國
革命成功以後可以做成一個獨立自
取國家民族的獨立自由爲的是希望
錢也都拿來獻給國家爲得是恨爭
海外用無數血汗換得來的有一個
所不計及至戰事發生以後、各位在
都拿田來翔助革命生命的繼牲也
海外用無數血汗換得來的有一個錢
孫先生領導之下、參加革命、各位在
京策動革命以來、愛闕的僑胞、就在
無限的感慨。
有無限的感慨諸位聽了也一定有
自從我們國父、孫中山先生、在東
兄弟現在東京向各位廣播心裡
愛國的海外備胞！
中華民國二十九年九月
對華廣播節目
和平反共建國運動。這一次國民政府
因此我們便在汪先生領導之下、展開
建設東亞、來恢復我們亞洲的地位
立自由、希望中日合作復興東亞
且還要以全力授助中國完成我們的獨
事並且尊重中國的獨立自由並
彙次表示、不但沒有滅亡中國的心
我們知道日本有了和平的意向、並且
外想辦法自從前年近衛聲明以後
能保得住國家民族的自由就得要分
段戰爭既然祇是無代價的牲不
家民族打算、職爭不過是不得已的手
了。
便被蔣介石及其親々戚々打光存光
連個們的子々孫々的一點一滴、也快
一天的破產、法幣一天一天的跌側
如今職事一天一天的拖延、經濟天
被蔣介石及其親々戚々打光存光了
了側們自己血汗所極來的一點一滴
都被蔣介石及其親々破々打光存光
個們祖宗血汗所遺下來的一點一滴
心的護他們的親々戚々大飽其私
華隊去屠殺儒們的兄弟姉妹、尤其痛
助共產黨去應爛側們的家鄉、縱容游
救國的結果呢？只是被轉介石來幫
難財便們把一切獻給國家是要來
更裏他的親々戚々上下其手、大發國
名行中飽之實、在戰本發生之後
以前他的親々戚々假建股之
權位、而作孤注一擲並且在職事發生
家民族一點一滴的積累爲他個人的
之後要努力於革命的建設他却拿國
須知道革命的目的便是要為國
日本放送協會
第五十號
國、復奚東亞。
勢力、實現和平、完成統復興中
介石、打倒共産為、打倒歐美的侵略
合起來保衛東亞。起來吧、打倒蔣
神來和以平等待我的民族日本聯
統一的惡勢力決門、拿備們的革命精
拿佩們的革命精神來和陳碍和平阻純
神來擁護和平反共建國的國民政府
命的熱情、起來吧、拿備們的革命精
備空着祖國、滿腔裡是愛國救國的革
努力於東亞新秩序的建股。同胞們！
中國立刻可以和日本聯合起來共同
成、全面的和平立刻可以實現、我們
的政策、合國的統一立刻可以完
戰、只要取慶方面放棄了容共抗日
的基礎已經確立祇要蔣介石停止抗
半的死路？政府還國民都之後、和平
救國、究竟走和平的生路、謙是走戰
平才是國家民族獨立生存的生路
住戰爭是國家民族毀滅的死路和
的如今只有和本才得回來才保得
東亞的誠意和熱情。
人見得日本對於中日平等合作復興
了東京、和日本朝野交換意見、更處
熱情、充分的表現川來、現在我們到
手調整邦交日本對於和平的駛意與
還都日本派遺大使到南京慶祝、
俯胞們一便們是愛國的、便們要
僑胞們！戰爭所要積性所要斷送

I have tro honor to refer to a report from the
Consulute dated November 20, 1939 entitled JAPANESE
SHORT AVE RADIO BRO. DC.STING FOR CVERSEAS CEPTICN
704.
and to my despatches Nos. 652 and 682 of May 21 and
July 23, 1940, resncctively concerning Japanese short
wave radio broaacasting for overseas reception in which
the attention of the Department was drawn to the fact
that while the Japanese authorities refuse to permit
FILFD
Japanese or foreigners fron owning short wave radio
receiving sets, they are makine every possible effort
to reach every part of the outside world with Govern-
ment controlled short wave propaganda - much of which
is frankly and officially directed to Japanese com-
munities in the United States and much is directed to
F/HC
the eneral merican public.
The
23

=== Page 98 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
The Department's attention is respeotfully called
to the radio broadoast on August 20, 1940 by Mr.
Takichiro Suma, Director of the Information Bureau of
the Japanese Foreign Office in Tokyo. A copy of the
address, as reported in the JAPAN TIMES & MAIL of August
21, 1940 is enclosed herewith. The speech was directed
solely to the merican public and is frankly and purely
Japanese Government official propaganda.
In view of the refusal of the Japanese Government
to permit moricans, other foreigners or the Japanese
public to operate short wave radio receiving sets and
to listen to merican short wave programs the action of
a high official of the Japanese Foreign Office in
making such use of the Jupanese short wave radio broad-
casting seems particularly inconsistent. Any state-
ments which he may make under such circunstances are
inevitably open to question since, as he admits, a
debate is not permitted.
The dissemination of Japanese propaganda especially
directed to the United States via the Government control-
led short wave broadcast stations appears to be growing
in volume.
Respectfully yours,
Rchord Bo
Richard F. Boyce,
American Consul.

The Tokyo Women's Christian College was' founded
in 1918 by the Christian mission of America and Canada
to contribute to the higher education of Japanese women
based on Christianity. At its initial period the university
was headed by the late Dr. Inazo Nitobé, an illustrious
worker for the betterment of America Japan relations.
and toJay. by Dr. Tetsu Yasui, a well-known women

=== Page 128 === (confidence 0.99)
The Japan Advertiser, Thursday, October 3, 1940.
RADIO TO BE EXPANDED
Transmitters to Be Added and For-
eign Broadcasts Increased
The Broadcasting Corporation of
Japan has revealed plans for a large-
scale expansion of its activities. accord-
ing to Domei
Two new 100-kilowatt transmitters
will be constructed. one in Osaka and
the other in Fukuoka. Kyushu. and
nine new local stations now under con-
struction will be added to the national
chain. The new local stations will be
in Toyohara. in Saghalein. Aomori.
Fukushima, Koriyama. Matsuyama.
Bofu, Onomichi, Oita and Okinawa.
It is also expected that a new station
will be built on the island of Palao. in
the South Sea Islands.
The number of foreign language used
in the foreign section of the corpora-
tion's activities will be increased from
12 to 16, and the total number of hours
given over to program raised fron 12
to 40.
A meeting will be held next month in
Taihoku. Formosa. to co-ordinate the
radio activities of Japan. China, Man-
hukuo and Formosa.
